F101 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/billing/billing.service.ts:274-279 + apps/api/src/config/configuration.ts:208 + apps/api/src/modules/entitlements/entitlement.service.ts:402 | A PAYING CUSTOMER CAN BE 403'd. The RevenueCat entitlement id is translated by `reverseEntitlementMap.get(raw) ?? raw` — an unmapped vendor id silently BECOMES our entitlement code. The grant is then written under that code, while `accessVerdict()` only ever asks about `this.defaultCode`. So a purchase whose RC entitlement identifier is not in REVENUECAT_ENTITLEMENT_MAP mints a live, paid grant the paywall never looks at. Config makes this likely, not hypothetical: the map DEFAULTS to a guessed pair `'premium:premium_monthly'`, and plans/payments-ideal-shape.md:196 contemplates the owner using `premium:premium` instead. | LADDER: compensates for not knowing RC's identifier at code-write time. Bedrock: the ledger is denominated in OUR vocabulary; a vendor id we cannot translate is an UNKNOWN, and minting a grant from an unknown is the same error class as `hasAccess` returning true on failure — inventing an assertion from absent information, which access-verdict.ts already rederived away one level up. Rederive: translation returns `mapped(code) | unknown(vendorId)`; `unknown` records the billing event FAILED (`unmapped_entitlement:<id>`) and rethrows so RC redelivers after the map is fixed; the legal code set is one closed declaration and `syncSubscriptionGrant` refuses anything outside it; the seeded default map value is deleted (unconfigured is unconfigured, not a guess). Red team: makes an invisible paid grant unrepresentable; still relies on the owner setting the map, but now fails LOUD (failed rows + retrying webhook) instead of a customer paying for a 403. | FIXED (D1 — owner-ratified 2026-08-02: adopt the shape) — translation is a SUM TYPE (billing/revenuecat-entitlement-map.ts): `mapped(code) | unknown(vendorId)`, no fallback. An unmapped RC id records the billing event FAILED as `unmapped_entitlement:<id>` and RETHROWS (RC redelivers); the same law now covers the TRANSFER/subscriber-fetch path, which carried the identical `?? rcId` passthrough. The guessed default map `'premium:premium_monthly'` is DELETED from configuration.ts — unconfigured means nothing translates. Parsing is STRICT and runs in the constructor, so a malformed map REFUSES BOOT (also refuses one vendor id mapping to two codes). The legal codes are one closed, derived union (default code + declared codes), exposed as `entitlementCodes`. PROOF: revenuecat-entitlement-map.spec.ts, 8 pass, incl. 'RED — an unmapped id records the event FAILED and RETHROWS' (asserts syncSubscriptionGrant NOT called, under any code) and 'an UNSET map translates nothing'. SURPRISE: billing-webhooks.spec.ts's harness defaulted entitlementMap to `''` and every test still translated — the F101 defect visible in the fixture itself; the harness now carries a real map. RESIDUAL CLOSED (owner-ruled 2026-08-03, D1-residual: PREMIUM IS THE ONLY PRODUCT, EVER). `RevenueCatEntitlementMap.parse` now REFUSES AT BOOT any entry whose `ourCode` differs from the default code, naming the offending code, the code accessVerdict() actually consults, and the single-product law. Consequence: the old 'one vendor id maps to two of our codes' check became unreachable (every surviving code IS the default) and was deleted rather than left as an untestable guard — its spec case feeds the same input and now hits the new refusal. SPEC: refusal + message content + happy path + default-code-relativity (the refusal is relative to config, not to the literal 'premium'); RED-proved by neutering the condition — 3 cases fail. | proof: entitlement.service.ts:402 reads only defaultCode; billing.service.ts:278-279 `?? rawEntitlement`; configuration.ts:208 seeded default.

F102 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/identity/auth/optional-clerk-auth.guard.ts:9 + apps/api/src/modules/entitlements/paywall-coverage.audit.ts:76-79 | THE BOOT AUDIT'S MARKER LIES BY OMISSION. `@BearsRequestUser()` asserts an unconditional fact, but OptionalClerkAuthGuard sets `request.user` ONLY when a token is present. The audit treats any marker-bearing guard as coverage, so a route guarded solely by OptionalClerkAuthGuard and lacking `@AllowUnentitled` PASSES boot — and then 403s every anonymous caller the moment ENTITLEMENT_GATING=enforce. That is precisely the incident class the audit exists to prevent. No live gap today (all five current OptionalClerkAuthGuard routes also carry @AllowUnentitled) — correct by discipline, not by construction. | LADDER: compensates for there being no static way to know whether a guard produces a user. Bedrock: "reachable anonymously" is a property of the ROUTE, not the guard, and guards come in three kinds, not two: always-authenticates, maybe-authenticates, never. A two-valued marker cannot express the middle one, so it rounds it to the dangerous value. Rederive: replace the boolean marker with `@AuthenticationEffect('required'|'optional'|'none')`; a route is covered iff some guard is 'required' OR it is @AllowUnentitled; an 'optional'-only route fails boot with "OptionalClerkAuthGuard admits anonymous callers — add @AllowUnentitled()". Red team: today's correct-by-luck pairing becomes unbypassable; a forgotten annotation defaults to 'none' and fails boot loudly; the only new failure is over-declaring 'optional' on a required guard, which forces a visible, harmless @AllowUnentitled. | FIXED (D2) — @AuthenticationEffect('required'|'optional'|'none') replaces the boolean marker; boot refuses an optional-only route naming it + the fix. Runtime behavior unchanged (boot-time only). PROOF: paywall-coverage.audit.spec.ts, 11 pass, incl. 'RED — an optional-only route admits anonymous callers and is reported', 'an optional-only route FAILS BOOT, naming the route and the fix', 'an optional guard PLUS @AllowUnentitled is covered, and boots'. user-bearing-guard.ts DELETED. | proof: executed spec (scratch, removed) — `bearsRequestUser(OptionalClerkAuthGuard) === true` and `uncoveredRoutes() === []` for a controller guarded only by a conditional marker-bearing guard; both assertions PASSED.

F103 | api-identity-money | apps/api/prisma/schema.prisma access_grants CHECK `access_grants_day_xor_absolute` | THE CONSTRAINT IS NAND, NOT XOR, AND THE COMMENT SAYS XOR. Live definition (verified against the local mirror): `CHECK (granted_days IS NULL OR expires_at IS NULL)` — it permits a row with BOTH null. entitlement.service.ts:108-110 documents it as "schema-enforced XOR". Both-null is how LIFETIME is spelled, so a day-grant row that lost its `granted_days` is indistinguishable from a lifetime grant: `deriveSummary` classifies `grantedDays === null` as absolute, finds `expiresAt === null`, and returns unbounded access. The emptiest possible row is the most powerful state. | LADDER: compensates for encoding three shapes in two nullable columns. Bedrock: there are three grant shapes (absolute-with-expiry, lifetime, day-count); a representation must make each one say its own name, and the accidental/empty state must be the LEAST powerful, never the most. Rederive: an explicit `kind` column ('absolute'|'lifetime'|'day') with a CHECK pinning each shape's columns exactly, so a row that sets nothing is illegal rather than immortal. A source-list-based CHECK is rejected: it would duplicate GRANT_POLICY in SQL. Red team: makes accidental-lifetime unrepresentable; costs a migration on money data; the existing code paths cannot currently produce both-null (grant() rejects days<=0 and demands expiresAt|lifetime), so this is a representation defect, not a live bug — which is exactly when it is cheap to fix. | FIXED (D3 — owner-ratified 2026-08-02) — migration 20260803020000_access_grant_kind adds `access_grants.kind` ('lifetime'|'days'|'window') with a per-kind CHECK (`access_grants_kind_shape`) pinning each shape's columns exactly; the NAND `access_grants_day_xor_absolute` is dropped. The backfill DERIVES kind from column shape (days-set→days, window-set→window, both-null→lifetime) and the migration then ASSERTS totality in a DO block — row count == classified count == count in the union, else RAISE and roll back. Code reads `kind` explicitly: deriveSummary partitions on `grant.kind`, the lifetime short-circuit tests `kind === 'lifetime'` rather than an absent expiry, and every write (grant, syncSubscriptionGrant create AND update) states its kind. APPLIED to the local mirror: comp→lifetime 2, subscription→window 2, trial_base→window 1, zero unclassified. Fresh-DB replay of all 218 migrations clean. PROOF: the DB-backed entitlement.service.spec.ts, 12 pass, incl. 'RED — a day-grant that loses its granted_days can no longer become immortal' (the DB rejects the both-null row with access_grants_kind_shape) and 'every write NAMES its shape'. | proof: `pg_get_constraintdef` on the local mirror.

F104 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/billing/billing.service.ts:184-188 | IDEMPOTENCY DEFEATED BY A FALLBACK. `externalId = event.id || original_transaction_id || transaction_id || `${Date.now()}`` — an event carrying none of the three ids gets a NEW synthetic id on every delivery, so the exactly-once check (`billingEventLog.findUnique`) can never match a replay of it, and RevenueCat's retries re-apply it. | LADDER: compensates for "the payload might not identify itself". Bedrock: an event without an identity cannot be deduplicated; a timestamp is not an identity, it is a guarantee of non-identity. Rederive: no id ⇒ 400 (or record FAILED with `unidentifiable_event`and rethrow) — refuse to process what you cannot recognise twice. Red team: makes silent duplicate application impossible; the new failure is rejecting a hypothetical id-less RC event, which is loud, findable, and correct. | FIXED (D4) — idempotency key = vendor event id, else a stable content hash of vendor-sent fields (billing/webhook-event-identity.ts); the Date.now() branch is deleted. PROOF: billing-webhooks.spec.ts 'an id-less event REPLAYED is applied exactly once (content-hash identity)' + MUTATION spec; mutation-verified RED — restoring`|| ${Date.now()}` makes the replay test fail with seen.size Expected 1 / Received 2 (the dup lands). | proof: read; the same file's own replay guard at :193-207 keys on this value.

F105 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/moderation/moderation.service.ts:35-43 | FAIL-OPEN MODERATION, WITH THE FIX ALREADY WRITTEN ELSEWHERE IN THE REPO. On any LLM error the decision is `{allowed:true, reason:'moderation_error'}` — a boolean return type that cannot say "I could not find out", so its catch block invents an assertion, globally, for every caller (username claims, poll/photo content). The comment concedes it and defers. | LADDER: this is verbatim the defect access-verdict.ts already rederived: three honest answers, not two. Bedrock: allowed / blocked / indeterminate; the policy for indeterminate belongs to the CALL SITE, because a username claim (retryable, cheap to refuse) and a comment post (availability wins) genuinely differ. Rederive: `ModerationVerdict = allowed | blocked(reason) | indeterminate(cause,message)` plus `resolveModeration(verdict, 'allow'|'hold')`, mirroring resolveVerdict exactly — the pattern is proven and tested in this codebase, it simply never reached moderation. Red team: makes a global lie unrepresentable; each surface must state its policy out loud; nothing changes behaviourally until a call site chooses 'hold'. | FIXED (D9 — owner-ratified 2026-08-02) — moderation-verdict.ts adopts the access-verdict shape verbatim: `allowed | blocked(reason) | indeterminate(cause,message)` plus `resolveModeration(verdict, 'allow'|'hold')`, documented in the same style and for the same reason. The catch block no longer invents `{allowed:true, reason:'moderation_error'}`; it logs the outage LOUD and returns indeterminate. ALL SEVEN call sites are WRITE paths and each states its policy out loud as `'hold'` with a per-site comment (polls: description, user-supplied entity name, poll title, discussion question, comment create, comment edit; identity: checkAvailability, which claimUsername uses as its only gate). A refusal now names its cause, so 'the text was rejected' and 'the moderator was unreachable' are distinguishable: username gained the reason `moderation_unavailable`, distinct from `profanity`. No read surface calls moderation today; 'allow' exists and is spec'd for when one does. PROOF: moderation-verdict.spec.ts, 7 pass, incl. 'RED — a moderator outage is indeterminate, NOT an allow'. | proof: read; access-verdict.spec.ts is the working precedent.

F106 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/identity/auth/clerk-auth.service.ts:59-135, 251-283 | 77 LINES OF DEFENSIVE PARSER FOR ONE ENV STRING, AND AN ABSENCE THAT GRANTS. `parseAudience` recursively accepts arrays, JSON arrays, single/double-quoted strings, comma lists, numbers, bigints and objects; `normalizeAudienceClaim` repeats the same taxonomy for the token side. Worse: when no audience is configured the check is SKIPPED with a `warn` (:52-56) — the very file that states "Absence of configuration must never grant access" (:294) for the dev token violates it for audience validation. | LADDER: compensates for nobody having decided the env var's format. Bedrock: an env var is a string and the owner picks ONE spelling; a parser that accepts every spelling is a decision deferred forever, and its breadth hides that the unconfigured case is the one that matters. Rederive: `value.split(',').map(trim).filter(Boolean)`; validate at boot — in prod, an unset CLERK_JWT_AUDIENCE fails startup rather than disabling a check silently. Deletes both functions' object/bigint/nested branches (~70 lines). Red team: makes "audience validation quietly off in a deployed environment" impossible; new failure is a boot refusal on a misconfigured box, which is the loud version of the same fact. | FIXED (D5) — unconfigured audience REFUSES: throws at boot in any non-dev APP_ENV, and verifyToken() refuses every token when unset (never skips). Parser reduced to split(',')+trim; the 77 lines of recursive/object/bigint branches and normalizeAudienceClaim's taxonomy are deleted (aud is string|string[] per RFC 7519). PROOF: clerk-auth.service.spec.ts, 6 pass, incl. 'a deployed environment refuses to BOOT' and 'RED — locally it boots, but every token is REFUSED (never skipped)'. | proof: read.

F107 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/entitlements/entitlement-enforcement.interceptor.ts:57-60 vs apps/api/src/modules/identity/user.service.ts:204 | THE GATING MODE IS PARSED TWICE, INDEPENDENTLY. The interceptor normalises `ENTITLEMENT_GATING` with trim+lowercase and a three-value whitelist; user.service re-reads `process.env` and re-normalises to answer `access.enforced` in the profile payload. Two readers, two parsers, one truth — the client's paywall routing axis and the server's actual behaviour can disagree if either changes. | LADDER: compensates for the mode having no home. Bedrock: one fact, one declaration; a mode that two subsystems must agree on belongs to the module that owns the mode. Rederive: `EntitlementService.gatingMode` (or a tiny exported `readGatingMode()` in entitlements) as the single reader; the interceptor and the profile payload both call it. Red team: eliminates drift; nothing else changes; the env var stays the switch. | FIXED (D6) — readGatingMode() in entitlements/entitlement-config.ts is the ONLY parser; the interceptor and user.service both call it. PROOF: entitlement-config.spec.ts lockdown 'ENTITLEMENT_GATING is parsed in exactly one file' + mode-parsing specs. Values unchanged. | proof: grep — the only two readers.

F108 | api-identity-money | entitlement.service.ts:137, billing.service.ts:54, user.service.ts:57, configuration.ts:211 | THE `|| 'premium'` DEFAULT IS WRITTEN FOUR TIMES. configuration.ts already defaults `billing.defaultEntitlement` to `'premium'`; three services then re-default it independently. Changing BILLING_DEFAULT_ENTITLEMENT's fallback in config leaves three services silently disagreeing. | LADDER: compensates for distrust of the config layer. Bedrock: a default is a value the OWNER chose once; repeating it is not defensiveness, it is three more places for it to be wrong. PROOF: entitlement-config.spec.ts lockdown "the 'premium' default literal is declared in config and NOWHERE else". Value byte-identical. | proof: grep.

F109 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/teaser/teaser.service.ts:127-131, 282, 425 | SEEDED PRIORS IN THE FIRST THING A USER EVER SEES. `MIN_MENTIONS = 3` and `MIN_RESULTS = 3` are tunables with no owner decision and no measurement behind them ("stands in for the unbuilt evidence floor"). `Math.min(row.score, 9.9)` is a display ceiling hard-coded twice in this file and defined nowhere central. | LADDER: compensates for the evidence floor not being built. Bedrock (owner law: numbers are facts, owner choices, or derivations): a floor that suppresses real results is a product decision, and a score ceiling is a property of the SCORE, not of the teaser. Rederive: the ceiling moves to wherever display_score is defined and is imported (one declaration); the mention floor becomes either the real evidence floor when built or an explicit owner constant with the choice recorded — and until then it is named as a placeholder in ONE place, not two thresholds that happen to both be 3. Red team: removes the possibility of the teaser and the search stack clamping differently. | FIXED (D7 — owner ruling 2026-08-02: "The only numbers the teaser screen should show is the typical numbers that show up in restaurant cards. Every other count or claim that's numeric should just be deleted.") — the 9.9 display ceiling was already deduplicated to TEASER_SCORE_CEILING (value byte-identical). This pass DELETED every other number from the teaser wire: `TeaserRow.mentionCount`, `TeaserRow.totalUpvotes`, `TeaserPreviewPayload.totalCount`, the `countConnections()` query that produced it, and the matching mobile types in apps/mobile/src/services/teaser.ts. What survives is the SCORE, read from core_public_entity_scores.display_score — the same column and the same path a real restaurant card uses. Deleting them from the payload (not just from the render) means no future surface can quietly start showing them; none of the three was rendered anywhere today. MIN_MENTIONS / MIN_RESULTS were CHECKED against the ruling's own test and KEPT: neither surfaces a user-visible number (MIN_MENTIONS is a WHERE-clause evidence floor, MIN_RESULTS a rung gate on the specificity ladder), so the ruled deletion does not reach them. They remain undeleted seeded priors and are re-escalated as such. PROOF: teaser-vocabulary.spec.ts 7 pass; tsc --noEmit green in apps/api AND apps/mobile. | proof: read; grep shows 9.9 appears in no non-spec file outside this one.

F110 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/teaser/teaser.service.ts:53-79 and :84-112 vs apps/mobile/src/constants/onboarding.ts | PARALLEL LISTS, AND A CROSS-APP VOCABULARY WITH NO SHARED HOME. `DISH_ID_TERMS` and `DISH_ID_LABELS` are two records keyed by the same eleven ids (the repo's own "type-list disease"): adding a dish means editing two literals and nothing catches a miss. Separately, every option id here (`fried-chicken`, `date-nights`, `mexican`, and the live-city names) is DECLARED by the mobile onboarding constants and RE-SPELLED here, with `packages/shared` sitting unused between them. A renamed option id degrades every teaser to the browse fallback silently — the fallback is what makes the drift invisible. | LADDER: compensates for the two apps not sharing a vocabulary module. Bedrock: an id crossing a network boundary is a contract, and a contract with two authors is a coincidence. Rederive: one record per dish `{terms, label}`; the option-id unions move to packages/shared and both apps import them, so a rename is a type error in mobile and a compile error in the API instead of a quiet downgrade. Red team: kills the silent-degradation path; costs one shared-package edit; the fallback stays for genuinely empty cities, where it is honest. | FIXED (D7) — the option-id vocabulary moved to packages/shared/src/constants/onboarding-vocabulary.ts (CRAVING/CONTEXT/CUISINE_OPTION_IDS + LIVE_CITY_VALUES); mobile BUILDS its option lists from it via labelledOptions() (a rename is a missing-label type error) and the teaser keys its tables by the unions (a rename is a compile error). DISH_ID_TERMS + DISH_ID_LABELS merged into ONE record per dish. PROOF: teaser-vocabulary.spec.ts (7 pass) + `tsc --noEmit` green in apps/api AND apps/mobile. | proof: grep — `apps/mobile/src/constants/onboarding.ts` and this file are the only two files carrying these ids.

F111 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/entitlements/entitlement.service.ts (GRANT_POLICY :33-40) vs access_grants.source in the local mirror | THE LEDGER HOLDS SOURCES THE CODE NO LONGER DECLARES. Two LIVE grants with `source = 'reward_photo'` exist in the local mirror; `reward_photo`/`reward_referral` were deleted from GRANT_POLICY on 2026-07-09. `deriveSummary` reads rows structurally (grantedDays/expiresAt), so these still grant access and `summarize().source` returns `'reward_photo'` — a string that reaches the client in `access.source` and that the product vocabulary no longer contains. | LADDER: compensates for `source` being an open text column while the code treats it as a closed union. Bedrock: if the union is the truth, the column must be the union (a Postgres enum or a FK to a source table); if the column is the truth, readers must handle unknown members explicitly. Rederive: enumerate the column and migrate the retired values to a retained `legacy_reward` member, so "what sources exist" has exactly one answer. Red team: prevents a retired source from reappearing in a client payload; migration touches money rows, hence escalate. NOTE: verified on the LOCAL mirror only — prod was not queried, per brief. | FIXED (D8 — owner ruling 2026-08-02: "we're doing away with this whole reward system for photos — get rid of that stuff.") — migration 20260803010000_abolish_photo_reward_grants DELETEs every `reward_photo` (and `reward_referral`) row from access_grants; the migration comment cites the ruling verbatim and states plainly that holders lose the coverage those rows conferred. SURPRISE, recorded for honesty: there was NO granting or display scaffolding left to remove. A repo-wide grep for reward_photo / photoReward / RewardPhoto over apps/, packages/ and scripts/ returned only DOC PROSE — schema.prisma's AccessGrant header still listed reward_photo/reward_referral as live sources, entitlement.service.ts's GRANT_POLICY header described them as merely deleted-from-code, user.service.ts's trial comment still said 'photo/invite rewards must be able to extend this', and two spec comments named the source. All corrected, so the source vocabulary now has ONE statement (GRANT_POLICY) and the table matches it. BANKING/REPLAY HUNT (before writing the migration): nothing keys off `source` except GRANT_POLICY and deriveSummary, which reads rows STRUCTURALLY (kind/grantedDays/expiresAt) and returns `source` only for display — so coverage after the delete is exactly the derivation over the remaining rows. PROVEN on the mirror: the DB-backed entitlement.service.spec.ts passes post-migration (12 tests), incl. a new 'the retired reward sources hold no rows anywhere in the ledger'. | proof: `select entitlement_code, source, count(*) ... from access_grants group by 1,2` on localhost/crave_search.

F112 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/identity/user.service.ts:498-502 (addDays) and apps/api/src/modules/entitlements/entitlement.service.ts:209-217 (findGrantByRef) | DEAD CODE, HUNTED BEFORE CLAIMING. `addDays` is a private method with zero references (grep across apps/api, apps/mobile, packages, scripts). `findGrantByRef` is a public service method with no caller anywhere in the repo — its doc calls it the "reward double-pay guard", and the reward sources it guarded were deleted 2026-07-09; the surviving guard is the (userId, source, sourceRef) unique index. | LADDER: both are residue of the deleted engagement-incentive machinery. The (userId, source, sourceRef) unique index remains the single idempotency contract. | proof: `grep -rn addDays\|findGrantByRef apps/api/src apps/mobile/src packages scripts` returns only the definitions.

F113 | api-identity-money | apps/api/src/modules/legal/legal.controller.ts:29-296, 310-585 | THE LEGAL DOCUMENTS ARE 550 LINES OF HTML COMPILED INTO THE API BINARY, WITH A HAND-MAINTAINED "Last Updated: January 6, 2026". Every wording change is a deploy; the date is a string nobody is forced to update; and the text asserts specifics that must track reality (advertising identifiers/IDFA collection, a named service-provider list, a 30-day deletion window that account-deletion.service.ts implements as immediate anonymization). A doc asserting what the code does, maintained separately from the code, drifts by default. | LADDER: compensates for having no static hosting. Rederive: the documents become files (or a docs route serving them) whose last-updated is derived from the file's own revision, not typed by hand. Red team: removes deploy-coupling and the lying-date failure; the wording itself remains the owner's to ratify. | PARTIAL (D10) — the hand-typed 'Last Updated: January 6, 2026' was written TWICE; it now derives from one LEGAL_LAST_UPDATED constant. NOTE: D10's premise that a 'single version constant already present' exists is FALSE — no legal/terms version constant exists anywhere in the repo; the constant was introduced here purely to stop the date being written twice. F200 | api-ops | ops-dashboard.controller.ts:41-53 (@Get page route) + ops-token.guard.ts:31 + ops-dashboard.html.ts:3,141-163 | THE OWNER'S INCIDENT CONSOLE CANNOT BE OPENED BY THE OWNER. The HTML page is served by `@Get()` under the class-level `@UseGuards(OpsTokenGuard)`, and that guard reads the `x-ops-token` HEADER only. A browser cannot attach a custom header to a top-level navigation, so `GET /ops` from a browser is a 401 in every case — with no token, with the documented `?token=` bootstrap, and with any URL a bookmark could hold. The page's own delivery machinery is therefore unreachable code: the module doc at html.ts:3 advertises "stores the ?token= in localStorage on first visit", and the `tokenGate` prompt at :152-162 is the fallback for a visit that can never happen. Only a header-capable client (curl/fetch) can fetch the page, and such a client has no use for HTML. This is the shipped consequence of a CORRECT fix applied at one level only: red team #7 removed the `?token=` branch because a query-string secret is recorded by the logging interceptor, the exception filter, Sentry, browser history and proxy logs — true, and it must not come back — but the PAGE's delivery was never rederived to match, so the surface was hardened into inaccessibility. | LADDER: the guard compensates for there being one credential mechanism for two genuinely different resources. Bedrock: the JSON routes carry DATA and are called by a header-capable client, so a header is the right credential; the PAGE carries no data at all and is fetched by a NAVIGATION, whose only credential mechanism the browser can supply automatically is a cookie. One guard cannot serve both without either leaking the secret (query) or refusing the browser (header) — which is exactly the fork this code is stuck on. Rederive: a one-time bootstrap route (`GET /ops/enter?token=…`) that validates, sets an HttpOnly+Secure+SameSite=Strict session cookie, and 302s to `/ops` WITHOUT the query string, so the secret never reaches a logged URL and never reaches JS; the page route accepts the cookie, the JSON routes accept cookie OR header; the page's localStorage token handling and tokenGate are then deleted outright (the browser holds the credential, not the document). Red team: makes "the console is unreachable in an incident" impossible and makes "the secret is in a logged URL" impossible at the same time — today's design only achieves the second. The bootstrap URL is still logged once, so it must be single-use-per-visit and the cookie short-lived; that is strictly better than every subsequent fetch carrying it. New failure: a cookie is ambient authority, so the JSON routes need the existing CSRF posture checked for the one POST (alerts ack). | FIXED (D19) | PROOF: executed Nest+Fastify probe, now PERMANENT as ops-browser-access.spec.ts (8/8 green). `GET /ops/enter?token=<correct>` => 302, Location == `/ops` carrying NEITHER the token NOR `token=`, Set-Cookie `ops_session=<expiry>.<hmac>` with HttpOnly+Secure+SameSite=Strict and NOT containing the secret; that cookie then opens BOTH `GET /ops` (200, real HTML) and `GET /ops/api/summary` (200). The three original defect assertions still hold as regression cases: naked `GET /ops` => 401, `GET /ops?token=<correct>` => 401 (the query-string secret stays dead), header => 200 UNCHANGED, and a PRESENT-but-wrong header still 401s (the cookie never widens a failed header check). OpsTokenGuard's file is byte-unchanged; OpsAccessGuard delegates to it whenever a header is present. Absent env => 404 on page, JSON and bootstrap alike. Forged, EXPIRED, and rotated-secret cookies all 401. Page's localStorage bootstrap + tokenGate DELETED — the document now holds no credential at all. NOTE FOR OWNER: `Secure` is unconditional per the binding constraint, so cookie access requires https (staging/prod); plain-http localhost must use the header path.

F201 | api-ops | ops-dashboard.controller.ts / ops-token.guard.spec.ts:45-67 | THE GUARD PROTECTING THE OPS CONSOLE HAD NO TEST THAT COULD SHOW RED ON THE COMPARISON. Three of the five cases ("401s a missing token", "401s a wrong token", "REFUSES a matching ?token= query param") supply the token via `query`, which a header-only guard ignores — so all three exercise the SAME branch, "no x-ops-token header". There was no case where a header was PRESENT but WRONG. MUTATION-PROVEN: with `constantTimeEquals` replaced by `return true`, so that any header value whatsoever opens the dashboard, the suite passed 5/5. The one thing the file exists to prove — that the secret is actually compared — was the one thing it could not fail on. Note this is the same disease CLAUDE.md's ratified methodology names ("an always-green metric is lying") and the same shape act-identity.ts documents one module over ("a test can assert a shared fragment EXISTS but not that the SQL is built from it"). | LADDER: compensates for a spec written against the OLD two-channel guard — when `?token=` was a live branch these cases were meaningful, and the security fix that deleted the branch silently converted three real tests into three copies of a fourth. Bedrock: a security test must vary the thing under test; a case that changes an IGNORED input is not a case. Rederive: assert the comparison directly — a wrong header value and a prefix-of-the-secret header value, both 401. | FIXED (test-only; production untouched) | PROOF: added the two cases; GREEN 7/7 on real code, RED 2/7 under the `constantTimeEquals -> true` mutant. Mutant reverted, `git diff` confirms only the spec changed.

F202 | api-signals | signal-demand-read.service.ts (14 occurrences of `LEFT JOIN entity_redirects` + `COALESCE(r.to_entity_id, …)`) vs :608-628 lastEntityViewAt; corroborated by signal-demand-read.territory.spec.ts:145 | THE LEDGER'S IDENTITY LAW IS A CONVENTION EVERY READER MUST REMEMBER, AND ONE READER ALREADY FORGOT. Because the ledger is never rekeyed (correct, and the reason merges are cheap), "resolve subject_id through entity_redirects at read" is load-bearing for correctness — and it is enforced nowhere. It is hand-copied as a LEFT JOIN + one-hop COALESCE at 14 sites in this file alone, plus demand-mass.reader.ts and poll-ballot-mention.service.ts. `lastEntityViewAt` omits it: it filters `s.subject_id = ${entityId}` raw, so after a merge the repeat-view dedupe valve stops seeing the loser-id history. Blast today is small (a duplicate view act inside the 2-minute window), but the omission is the PROOF that nothing prevents the next, larger one — the next reader to forget could be a demand-mass path. The guard that exists is worse than none: territory.spec.ts:145 asserts `expect(demandSql).toContain('entity_redirects')`, which a reader that joins the table and then ignores the COALESCE passes, and which act-identity.ts's own header already names as the failure mode that let three SQL dialects coexist under a shared vocabulary. | LADDER: compensates for `signals`/`signal_demand_daily` exposing a RAW subject_id that is not the identity anyone wants. Bedrock: if no legitimate reader ever wants the unresolved id, the unresolved id should not be what a reader can reach — the resolution belongs to the substrate, not to each caller's memory. This is precisely the class the extraction-scope lockdown solved for events, and the same move act-identity.ts already made one level up: `dailyActsCteSql` became a BUILDER exactly because "a builder cannot be half-adopted" — yet the redirect join is still passed INTO it by each caller via the `from` parameter, so the one abstraction that closed this hole for the day-acts law deliberately left identity outside it. Rederive: a database VIEW (`signals_resolved` / `signal_demand_daily_resolved`) exposing `subject_id` already folded through the one-hop redirect, with the raw columns renamed (`raw_subject_id`) so reaching for the unresolved id is a deliberate, greppable act; every reader selects from the view, and the app-side `expandWithRedirectSources` sargability trick becomes an index on the view's predicate rather than a thing each query restates. Where a view cannot serve (the aggregate REBUILD must write raw ids), the raw table stays reachable under its own name — one place, stated. Red team: makes "a reader forgot the COALESCE" unrepresentable rather than merely untested; the `toContain` test can then be deleted rather than strengthened, because there is no fragment left to assert. New failure: a view adds a planner hop on hot autocomplete reads — must be measured against the current sargable-expansion path before adopting, and if it loses, the fallback is the weaker-but-real fix (make `dailyActsCteSql` own the redirect join instead of accepting it as a parameter, and route every non-CTE reader through a single `resolvedSubjectSql()` builder). | FIXED (D20a — the fragment-home fallback, NOT the view) | PROOF: new signals/subject-identity.ts owns `redirectJoinSql` / `resolvedSubjectSql` / `subjectMatchesSql`; all 14 hand-copied sites in signal-demand-read.service.ts converted, and the LIVE OMISSION at lastEntityViewAt fixed (it now joins and compares through `subjectMatchesSql`, so a merged loser id's history is visible again). subject-identity-lockdown.spec.ts is MUTATION-PROVEN: planting a hand-rolled `COALESCE(r.to_entity_id, s.subject_id)` + `LEFT JOIN entity_redirects` in a scratch file turned it RED naming `modules/signals/scratch-mutation-probe.ts`; probe removed, 7/7 green. The weak `toContain('entity_redirects')` assertions are gone. The VIEW was deliberately not adopted: its planner-hop cost on the hot autocomplete path is unmeasured, and F202 itself names this as its fallback.

F203 | api-signals | signals.service.ts:160-180 (record) + 7 injecting services | "RECORD THE SIGNAL" IS A CONVENTION, NOT A CHOKEPOINT — AND THE LEDGER IS THE ONE RECORD (§3). `record()` returns `void` and is called as a bare fire-and-forget statement by 7 services (search, on-demand-request, search-query-interpretation, polls, history, user-lists, signals.controller). Nothing structurally connects an ACT to its ledger row: a new surface that performs a user act simply does not call it, and the omission is invisible forever — no test fails, no metric moves, and the aggregate faithfully derives from a ledger that never heard about the act. The controller's own doc states the intended law ("every other act kind records at its own chokepoint") but "chokepoint" here means "a line of code someone remembered to write at the right place", which is the same level the codebase has repeatedly rejected elsewhere. HONESTLY: I found no MISSING writer today — the 7 sites cover the 9 declared kinds. This is a representation defect, not a live bug, which per this repo's own precedent (F103's framing) is exactly when it is cheapest to fix. | LADDER: compensates for the act and its record being two separate things a developer must pair. Bedrock: the ledger is the ONE record of an act, so an act that is not recorded is not an act — the pairing should be structural, not remembered. Rederive (two candidate shapes, both weaker than I would like — flagging the problem more confidently than the solution): (a) type-level — the operations that constitute acts return an `Act<K>` token that only `record()` consumes, so an unrecorded act is an unused value a lint rule can fail on; (b) boot-audit — the same shape as the paywall-coverage audit that already exists in this repo (entitlements/paywall-coverage.audit.ts): enumerate the routes that mutate user-visible state, require each to declare either a `@RecordsSignal('kind')` or an explicit `@NoSignal('reason')`, and refuse to boot on an undeclared one. (b) is the proven local pattern and I would start there. Red team on (b): it makes the omission LOUD at boot instead of silent forever, and forces the reason for a non-recording route to be written down; it does NOT prove the recorded kind is the RIGHT kind, and a lazy `@NoSignal('n/a')` defeats it — so the declaration must be reviewed like any other, and the audit is a floor, not a ceiling. | FIXED (D20b — option (b), the paywall-coverage-audit shape) | PROOF: @RecordsSignal(kind)/@NoSignal(reason) + SignalCoverageAudit wired into SignalsModule. A USER-ACT ROUTE is structural: mutating verb + a guard declaring @AuthenticationEffect('required') + not @AllowUnentitled. BOTH DIRECTIONS proven by signal-coverage.audit.spec.ts (5/5): an undeclared act route throws at boot naming the route AND the fix; either declaration passes; an EMPTY @NoSignal reason is NOT a declaration; a GET, a public route and an operator route are not asked; and controllers-with-zero-act-routes throws the not-vacuously-green error. EXECUTED census over the REAL controllers (reflect-metadata probe, removed): 53 user-act routes, 0 UNDECLARED, and all 9 declared kinds mapped faithfully to the 7 recording services — search/autocomplete_selection/on_demand_ask at SearchController.run+runNatural, search at recordCacheAttribution, entity_view at both HistoryController writes, favorite_added at UserListsController.addFavoriteItem+addListItem, poll_created/poll_comment/poll_vote at PollsController, viewport_dwell at SignalsController. Zero behavior change: declarations are metadata only.

F204 | api-signals | integrity/sybil-cluster-report.service.ts:606 | THE ONE PLACE IN THE REPO THAT COMPARES `signals.occurred_at` WITHOUT THE COERCION THE MODULE WROTE FOR IT. `AND s.occurred_at >= now() - make_interval(days => 30)` compares a NAIVE-UTC column against a timestamptz, so Postgres coerces the right side to the SESSION time zone's wall clock. signals/sql-instant.ts exists for precisely this column and this reason, and every other consumer uses it. Measured on the local mirror (session TZ America/Chicago, `occurred_at` confirmed `timestamp without time zone`): the boundary is `2026-07-03 20:39` under Chicago vs `2026-07-04 01:39` under UTC — the sybil sweep's 30-day lookback silently slides by the server's UTC offset. Correct on a UTC box, wrong by the offset anywhere else, and the asymmetry is nasty: Prisma parses the same column back into JS as UTC (verified), so the JS-side timing math is right while the SQL-side window is skewed. | LADDER: compensates for nothing — it is a helper that nobody is FORCED to use, which is the same class as F202 one module over. Bedrock: a naive-UTC column has exactly one correct comparison form, and the module that owns the column already states it. Rederive: `AND s.occurred_at >= ${utcInstantSql(new Date(Date.now() - SWEEP_LOOKBACK_DAYS * MS_PER_DAY))}` — the import is one line. Structurally: sql-instant.ts's doc says "for signals.occurred_at, and nothing else"; a lint rule (or the existing source-scanner pattern that policed 162 columns before it was retired) keyed on the literal `occurred_at` appearing in raw SQL without `utcInstantSql` would make the omission impossible rather than merely rare — worth it precisely because there is now exactly one column left to police. | FIXED (D21) — NOTE: the one-line utcInstantSql fix had ALREADY landed at :606 under a prior pass before I arrived; what this pass added is the POLICE. | PROOF: executed on localhost/crave_search — `SHOW timezone` => America/Chicago; `pg_typeof(occurred_at)` => timestamp without time zone; the two boundary values above computed under `SET TIME ZONE` Chicago vs UTC; Prisma round-trip probe confirmed the JS side reads UTC.

F205 | api-signals + api-ops | signal-demand-aggregate.service.ts:201-207, signal-partition-maintenance.service.ts:78-86, integrity/sybil-cluster-report.service.ts:116-122 | THREE CRONS WHOSE DEATH IS INVISIBLE, ONE OF WHICH SILENTLY STOPS THE LEDGER. All three catch everything and emit a log line only; none emits an ops_alert, though OpsAlertsService is the codebase's single owner-attention seam, is already injected into the sybil service, and is described in its own doc as existing for exactly these chokepoints. The consequences are not equal and the partition one is severe: partition maintenance keeps `signals` RANGE partitions ahead of the clock, and its own header states that an insert into a month with no partition FAILS the write while the §3 law makes the writer swallow that failure — so a dead cron eventually means every signal is silently dropped, observable only as a `warn` per write and a ledger that quietly stops growing. Its comment calls the failure "loud but non-fatal"; `logger.error` with no alert is not loud, and the two-month lead means the alarm the design relies on has no bell. Aggregate refresh failing silently leaves the collector's territory read deciding what to enrich from a frozen aggregate. Sybil sweep failing silently turns the vote-integrity detector off with no indication — an always-green instrument by absence. | LADDER: each catch compensates for a real and correct law ("a background failure must not crash the process"). Bedrock: not crashing and not telling anyone are different decisions, and the second was never made — it was inherited from the first. The repo has already ratified the principle ("every metric must be able to show RED") and already built the mechanism. Rederive: each catch keeps swallowing AND calls `opsAlerts.emit({severity, kind, dedupeKey})` — `critical` for partition maintenance (it can lose user acts), `warn` for the other two, each with a dedupeKey so a persistent failure nags once rather than every tick, which the emit path already supports. Better still for the partition case, because a cron that never RUNS raises no exception to catch: assert the invariant instead of the pass — a cheap daily check that a partition covering `now + 1 month` exists, alerting on its absence, which catches a scheduler that died as well as a DDL that failed. Red team: the alert path itself can fail, and it too is fire-and-forget — so this reduces silence, it does not eliminate it; the partition-existence assertion is the one that genuinely cannot be fooled by a process that simply stopped scheduling. | FIXED (D21) | PROOF: all three catches now swallow AND emit, with day-bucketed dedupeKeys per the existing pattern — partition maintenance CRITICAL (`signal_partition_maintenance_failed:<day>`), aggregate refresh WARN, sybil sweep WARN. PLUS the un-foolable shape: `assertLeadPartitionExists()` runs daily and alerts CRITICAL when the now+1-month partition is absent, which fires on a scheduler that simply stopped as readily as on a DDL that threw. LIVE PROOF on the local mirror inside BEGIN/ROLLBACK: the assertion's exact query returned 1 with `signals_p2026_09` present, 0 after DETACH+DROP of that future partition, and 1 again after ROLLBACK (mirror untouched). Unit-proven both ways in signal-partition-maintenance.service.spec.ts (silent when present, CRITICAL when absent, WARN when the check itself throws).

F206 | api-ops | ops-summary.service.ts:253-261 (warnSwallowed) + :704-737, :777-797, :846-884 | THE INCIDENT DASHBOARD CANNOT SAY "I DON'T KNOW", SO IT SAYS ZERO. `warnSwallowed` is a good single seam and its doc states the right rule ("degrade its own card, but NEVER silently — every swallow logs"). But logging is not the payload: a failed `placesSection` returns `[]`, a failed `sourcesSection` returns `[]`, and each failed pipeline count returns `0`. On screen an empty Sources card and `docs24h: 0` are indistinguishable from a genuinely idle pipeline — the reading most likely to be WRONG at the exact moment the operator is looking, since the DB read failing is itself the incident. The honest shape is already in this very file, twice: `drainPending: null` renders "—" with the comment "an honest '—' beats a fabricated number", and the TomTom credit block returns `declared: false` with every field null rather than inventing a balance. The rule was derived and applied to the fields where the ABSENCE was known at design time, and not to the fields where absence arrives at runtime. | LADDER: compensates for the payload type modelling every cell as a number/array with no third state. Bedrock: a dashboard cell is one of {measured value, not measured} — the same three-honest-answers move access-verdict.ts and (per F105) moderation already needed. A zero is a measurement. Rederive: each section returns `{ok: true, data} | {ok: false, reason}`, and the renderer prints "—" plus a failed-section marker for the second, exactly as it already does for drainPending. Red team: makes "the dashboard showed green while the DB was down" impossible; costs a payload shape change and matching renderer edits in ops-dashboard.html.ts; no behaviour changes on the happy path. Related, same file: `dayBucket` (:432-437) clamps the bucket index into [0, 29], so a row timestamped at the window edge is folded into the last bucket rather than dropped or flagged — harmless for a 30-day chart, noted for completeness. | FIXED (D22) | PROOF: `SectionResult<T> = {ok:true,data} | {ok:false,reason}` now wraps places, sources and all six pipeline counts; the renderer prints an honest dash plus the failure reason (`sectionOk`/`numSection`/`sectionFailedHtml`), following the in-file drainPending precedent. drainPending stays `number|null` — absence known at DESIGN time keeps its design-time shape. Spec-proven: a failed Places/Sources read yields `{ok:false,reason}` and NOT `{ok:true,data:[]}`; a failed count yields `{ok:false}` while its neighbours stay `{ok:true,data:7}`; and the renderer spec asserts every pipeline cell routes through numSection and none through num().

F207 | api-signals | signal-demand-read.service.ts:1070-1078 (expandWithRedirectSources) and :1111-1118 (expandPlaceIdsWithAncestors) | TWO CATCH BLOCKS THAT CHANGE THE ANSWER, NOT THE AVAILABILITY. Both are described as pure sargability/expansion optimisations, but their fallbacks return the UNEXPANDED input: on a transient DB error `expandWithRedirectSources` drops every merged entity's history from the demand score, and `expandPlaceIdsWithAncestors` drops the ancestor rows that the read algebra's own doc calls load-bearing ("a coarse signal stored at TX reaches an Austin engine through the TX row"). The caller receives a smaller number with no indication it is smaller, and `logger.debug` — not warn, not error — is the only trace. These numbers feed the collector's decision about what gets enriched, i.e. real spend. | LADDER: compensates for wanting a demand read never to fail. Bedrock: an under-counted demand score is not a degraded answer, it is a WRONG answer, and the two are not interchangeable when the consumer spends money on the result. The expansion is not an optimisation — the SQL's fold-back COALESCE is semantically complete only over the expanded set, so a failed expansion changes semantics. Rederive: let both throw; the CALLER decides whether a demand read that cannot be computed should fail the request or be skipped this cycle — the same "policy belongs at the call site" conclusion the repo reached for moderation. If a fallback must exist, it must be visible in the RETURN (a `partial: true` flag), never only in a debug log. Red team: makes silent under-counting unrepresentable; new failure is a demand read that now surfaces an error a caller must handle, which is the loud version of the same fact. Note `resolveActorId` (:1127-1141) has the same shape but is honest: no actor genuinely means no personal history, so returning null there is correct — the contrast is why the other two stand out. | FIXED (D22) | PROOF: both fallbacks DELETED — expandWithRedirectSources and expandPlaceIdsWithAncestors throw now (spec-proven: `rejects.toThrow('connection reset')` / `('statement timeout')`). COLLECTOR CALL SITE FOUND at keyword-slice-selection.service.ts:245 (KeywordSliceSelectionService.selectTermsForSource -> territoryEntityDemand), and it implements skip-this-cycle-LOUDLY: logger.error + an ops alert WARN `collector_demand_read_unavailable:<sourceId>:<day>` naming the source, then rethrow. The rethrow reaches CollectorPacerService's existing per-lane catch, which leaves the lane DUE — so the cycle is skipped with no cadence advance, rather than resolving with `terms: []`, which the pacer reads as the legitimate 'nothing due' outcome. Spec keyword-slice-demand-unavailable.spec.ts proves both (2/2).

F208 | api-ops | infrastructure/throttler/throttler.decorator.ts:12,26-36,98-102,115-132 | A DECORATOR THAT DECLARES A CEILING AND APPLIES NOTHING, PLUS FOUR DEAD EXPORTS. (a) `tierLimits` has no `default` entry, so `@RateLimitTier('default')` — used on 4 routes including this territory's own `POST /signals/viewport-dwell` — pushes no `Throttle()` at all and only sets metadata. The route falls through to the global ceiling, which is the intended OUTCOME, but the annotation reads as a decision that was made and is in fact inert; the day someone adds a `default` entry, four routes change ceiling silently. (b) `RATE_LIMIT_TIER_KEY` metadata is SET on every tiered route and READ by nothing — the real mechanism is Nest's own `Throttle()`. (c) `SKIP_THROTTLE_KEY` and (d) the exported `SkipThrottle` wrapper have zero usages anywhere; `SkipThrottle` survives only in throttler.module.ts's doc prose. (e) the `premium` tier carries a full limit set and has never been applied to a route — and cannot be, since `RateLimitTier` takes a literal and nothing consults entitlement state. DEAD-CODE HUNT COMPLETED before claiming: repo-wide grep over apps/api, apps/mobile, packages, scripts for each symbol; no dynamic/computed `RateLimitTier(` call site exists; `git log -S` shows `premium` unused since its introducing commit (1d6a7fd97); the one other worktree is an unrelated detached deploy-retry checkout. | LADDER: (a) compensates for wanting every route to state its tier even when the tier is the default — a real and good instinct. Bedrock: an annotation should either apply something or say something a reader can act on; "default" applies nothing and says only what the absence of an annotation already says. Rederive: either give `default` an explicit entry equal to today's global values, so the annotation is true and the global config stops being an invisible second source of ceilings, or drop the `'default'` member and let its absence mean the global tier. I prefer the first — it makes every route's ceiling readable at the route. (b)-(d) delete (~10 lines, two exported symbols); (e) delete the `premium` tier until an entitlement-aware tracker exists to select it, since a limit nothing can reach is a claim about a future nobody has committed to. | FIXED (D23) | PROOF: greps re-verified at implement time (RATE*LIMIT_TIER_KEY, SKIP_THROTTLE_KEY, SkipThrottle, premium — all self-referencing only, plus stale dist/); `default` now carries an explicit entry equal to config's global values, `tierLimits` is a TOTAL Record so no member can be inert, and RateLimitTier always pushes Throttle(); the four dead symbols and the premium tier are DELETED. throttler-tiers.spec.ts asserts the default tier equals `configuration().throttler` and that every declared tier applies three positive windows. TRADE RECORDED: the default tier is now literals, so THROTTLER*\*\_LIMIT env vars no longer move those 4 routes (they still govern every unannotated route). Original greps: `@RateLimitTier` usage census (7 sensitive, 5 search, 5 heavyGeoRead, 4 default, 3 webhook, 2 publicRead, 2 naturalSearch, 1 autocomplete, 1 auth, 0 premium).

F209 | api-ops | infrastructure/throttler/throttler.module.ts:15-22 and throttler.decorator.ts:14-24 | COMMENTS ASSERTING STALE NUMBERS ON THE FILE THAT DECIDES EVERY CEILING. The module header states "short: 3 requests per 1 second, medium: 20 requests per 10 seconds, long: 100 per 60"; the live defaults in config/configuration.ts:152-167 are 5, 30, 100. Separately, every member of the `RateLimitTierName` union carries a trailing req/min comment ("search // 60 req/min", "autocomplete // 120 req/min", "naturalSearch // 30 req/min", "premium // 300 req/min") and NONE matches `tierLimits` twenty lines below (search is 25/120/1200 across three windows; there is no single req/min figure to state). A developer choosing a tier reads the comment, not the table. The module header also says "Excluded from rate limiting: /health only" while the set holds three paths — all health probes, so the claim is true in spirit and I am not counting it as a defect. | LADDER: compensates for nothing; the numbers moved and the prose did not. Bedrock: a number that lives in code should not be restated in prose beside it — the restatement is a second source of truth with no mechanism to keep it honest. Rederive: delete the numeric claims from both comment blocks and let the tier table and the config block speak; keep the WHY comments (the heavyGeoRead and webhook rationales are excellent and irreplaceable — they record measured facts and an incident, which prose is the right home for). | FIXED (D23) — stale numeric claims deleted from both comment blocks; the heavyGeoRead/webhook/publicRead WHY prose (measured facts + an incident) kept verbatim. | PROOF: read both files against config/configuration.ts:152-167.

F210 | api-ops | infrastructure/throttler/throttler.module.ts:114-126 | FALSY-ZERO IN A CEILING: `configService.get<number>('throttler.short.limit') || 5`. A configured limit of `0` — the only way to spell "this window is closed" — is discarded and silently becomes 5, for all three windows. Identical in class to F114 (rate-limit-coordinator discarding an operation limit of zero) and to the spend-cap trap `readSpendCapUsd` was written to kill. Latent today (config supplies parsed env with non-zero defaults, so the `||` never fires), which is exactly when it is cheap. | LADDER: compensates for "the config value might be missing". Bedrock: a limit is one of {positive ceiling, 0 = closed, absent = use the default}; `||` cannot distinguish the second from the third. Rederive: `??` plus a boot-time validation that the parsed value is a finite non-negative integer — a malformed ceiling should refuse to boot rather than quietly widen. Note the config layer already owns these defaults (:152-167), so the `|| N` literals here are also a second declaration of the same numbers, the F108 pattern. Red team: nothing new can fail; a typo'd 0 now closes a window loudly instead of opening it to 5. | FIXED (D23) | PROOF: `readThrottlerWindow()` uses `??`-semantics explicitly — a configured 0 is KEPT (spec: `{ttl:1000,limit:0}`), a malformed value (NaN, i.e. `parseInt('abc')`) THROWS in the module factory so boot refuses, and an absent value throws too since config already owns the defaults. The `|| N` literals are gone, so the numbers are declared once, in configuration.ts.

F211 | api-ops | debug/debug.module.ts:9-15 | A STALE COMMENT ASSERTING THAT FINISHED WORK IS UNFINISHED. The header warns "⚠️ This module should be conditionally loaded only in development/staging, NOT in production!" and then instructs the reader how to achieve it ("1. Use a ConfigService check in app.module.ts / 2. Or check NODE_ENV before importing"). It IS conditionally loaded: app.module.ts:123 registers it only when `isDebugRoutesEnabled()`, a gate with its own spec (shared/config/debug-routes.gate.spec.ts) that refuses `prod` outright. The debug CONTROLLER's own comment states this correctly. A TODO that survives its own completion trains readers to distrust the warnings that are still real — and invites someone to "fix" a hole that is already closed. | LADDER: no compensation; residue. Bedrock: the gate is the statement; the comment should describe it, not demand it. Rederive: replace the warning with a one-line pointer to the gate and its spec. | FIXED (D23 group) — the warning+TODO replaced by a pointer to isDebugRoutesEnabled() and its spec. | PROOF: app.module.ts:123 + shared/config/debug-routes.gate.ts:23 + its spec, all read.

F212 | api-signals | integrity/sybil-cluster-report.service.ts:38-40 vs :558-587 | THE COMMENT SAYS THE SWEEP IS BOUNDED; TWO OF ITS THREE QUERIES ARE NOT. `SWEEP_LOOKBACK_DAYS` is documented as "K7 plumbing — sweep lookback. Bounds the daily scan", and it bounds exactly one fetcher (`fetchIpVoteRows`). `fetchDeviceVoteClusters` joins `user_devices` to ALL of `poll_endorsements` with no time predicate, and `fetchHeavyDevices` scans all of `user_devices` — both grow without limit for the life of the product, and both re-report the same ancient clusters forever (harmless only because the ops_alerts dedupe key collapses them). Downstream, `filterToLiveEndorsements` and `leaderWithAndWithout` then issue one `pollEndorsement.findMany` PER POLL, twice over — an N+1 across an unbounded poll set. | LADDER: the constant compensates for wanting a bounded daily job; it was applied to the query it was written next to. Bedrock: "the sweep looks back N days" is a property of the SWEEP, so every fetcher it drives must honour it. Rederive: push the lookback into all three fetchers (device clusters and heavy devices bounded by endorsement/registration recency), and collapse the two per-poll endorsement loops into one keyed fetch for the whole poll set. Red team: a genuinely old ring that only votes today is still caught, because the ENDORSEMENT is recent even when the device registration is not — so bound on the act, not on the registration, or the fix creates a real blind spot. That subtlety is the reason to design this rather than patch it. | FIXED (D24) | PROOF: all three fetchers bounded, on the ACT. fetchDeviceVoteClusters adds `pe.created_at >= <cutoff>` while the multi-account-device subquery stays DELIBERATELY unbounded, so an old ring voting today is still caught — spec-asserted directly (`toContain('pe.created_at >=')`, `not.toContain('ud.first_seen')`, `not.toMatch(/ud\.last_seen\s*>=/)`). fetchIpVoteRows already carried it. DEVIATION, FLAGGED: fetchHeavyDevices is bounded on recent endorsement OR recent device activity (a UNION), because a pure endorsement bound would delete the K1 sentence's 'votes or not' trigger for a brand-new device — the union bounds the scan without narrowing what the sentence catches, and has no blind spot in either direction. The N+1 is collapsed into ONE keyed `findMany({where:{pollId:{in:[...]}}})` shared by filterToLiveEndorsements and leaderWithAndWithout via a per-sweep EndorsementIndex; spec proves 3 polls => 1 call, and the fake prisma now understands ONLY `in` so a regression cannot hide. 14/14 green.

F213 | api-ops | ops-summary.service.ts:21-26 (DAILY_WINDOW_DAYS) | A SELF-ADMITTED SECOND COPY OF ONE NUMBER. `DAILY_WINDOW_DAYS = 30`, whose own comment says it "mirrors spend-analytics.service.ts's UNIT_COST_WINDOW_DAYS (same 30-day horizon, independently named here since this module has no dependency on that service's internal constant)". The file already imports `median` and the pricing tables from that exact module, so the stated reason — no dependency — is not true. The dashboard's chart window and the unit-cost window drifting apart would make the displayed "expected" line quietly incomparable to the governance numbers derived from the other. | LADDER: compensates for a constant not being exported. Bedrock: one horizon, one declaration; if two subsystems must agree on it, it belongs to whichever owns the concept (spend-analytics). Rederive: export `UNIT_COST_WINDOW_DAYS` and import it, exactly as `median` already is. Red team: nothing new can fail; drift becomes impossible. | FIXED (D24) | PROOF: `UNIT_COST_WINDOW_DAYS` exported from spend-analytics.service.ts and imported in ops-summary.service.ts alongside the `median` that was already imported; `DAILY_WINDOW_DAYS = UNIT_COST_WINDOW_DAYS`. The untrue justification comment is replaced by the reason drift is now impossible.

F214 | api-signals | ARGUED IDEAL — recorded so a later pass does not re-litigate | Four shapes in this territory are the right shape and should be defended, not "improved". (1) signals.service.ts's write path: `record()` exposing ONLY create, with no update/delete by law, is what makes the append-only claim structural rather than procedural; its double catch (async + sync) is not belt-and-braces but the honest implementation of "never throws into the caller", and the normalize-geo-before-early-return at :311-313 fixes a real unhandled-rejection path. (2) act-identity.ts's `dailyActsCteSql` BUILDER: the header argues explicitly that a half-adoptable helper is what let three dialects of one law coexist, and its `subjectScope` doc is a model of honesty — it states that removing the clause changes no reader's output and that it is kept for scan cost, NOT correctness, while naming the two clauses (kind grain, echo exclusion) that ARE load-bearing. That is a constant documenting its own blast radius. (3) The ops_alerts emitter question in the brief: there is ONE seam, not eight copies — every emitter across the codebase calls `OpsAlertsService.emit()`, which owns dedupe collapse, the email-only-on-new-insert rule, and the shutdown pending-set. Nothing to rederive. (4) ops-token.guard.ts's own logic (as distinct from its spec, F201, and its page-delivery consequence, F200): absent env => 404 rather than 401 is the correct distinction between "off" and "unauthorised", and the constant-time comparison with its length-leak note is right and correctly reasoned. | - | - | read + the mutation and reachability evidence above.

F300 | api-migrations | apps/api/prisma/migrations/** (215 migration.sql + migration_lock.toml) | REPLAYABILITY VERDICT: **PASS\*\*. `prisma migrate deploy` against a freshly-created empty database (`createdb crave_replay_test`, DATABASE_URL override) applied ALL 215 migrations end-to-end — "All migrations have been successfully applied", background exit code 0. Post-state verified independently: `_prisma_migrations` = 215 rows with finished_at NOT NULL; 97 tables + 2 non-app (spatial_ref_sys, \_prisma_migrations) in public; 7 extensions present (plpgsql, btree_gin, pg_trgm, fuzzystrmatch, citext, postgis, vector). No function/extension is referenced before creation, no data-dependent migration assumes rows exist (every backfill is a set-based UPDATE/INSERT ... SELECT that no-ops on empty), and the crave_fold create(20260802010000)→use(20260802020000_identity_key_sorted)→drop(20260802050000) sequence replays clean. Scratch DBs dropped after the run. | IDEAL-VERIFIED (historical artifacts, replay-proven) | executed: createdb + prisma migrate deploy + psql verification, then dropdb

F301 | api-migrations | 20260720030000_phase_c_purge/migration.sql:21-23 | **THREE ORPHANED ENUM TYPES — a silent no-op DROP.** The Phase C purge wrote `DROP TYPE IF EXISTS "SearchEventKind"; DROP TYPE IF EXISTS "DemandSourceKind"; DROP TYPE IF EXISTS "DemandSignalKind";` — Prisma MODEL-style PascalCase, quoted. The actual Postgres types are snake_case: `search_event_kind` (created 20260609120000_capture_db_push_drift:2), `demand_source_kind` and `demand_signal_kind` (created 20260514211500_search_demand_daily:3,10). `IF EXISTS` turned all three misses into silent successes, so the types survive on every database. PROVEN on the replay DB: all three exist with 0 columns referencing them (`pg_attribute` count = 0); zero references in apps/api/src or schema.prisma; and `prisma migrate diff` independently reports them as `[-] Removed enums`. Sibling `demand_subject_kind` correctly has 2 live columns and must NOT be dropped. Genuinely dead, non-data-bearing (an enum type holds no rows), so a forward DROP is safe and needs no owner escalation for data loss. | ESCALATED (design proposal — new forward migration) | psql pg_type/pg_attribute on replay DB + grep(src, schema.prisma) + prisma migrate diff

F302 | api-migrations | prisma/schema.prisma model PhotoEvent (eventType) vs 20260710120000_photos_hardening | **REAL SCHEMA/DB TYPE DRIFT.** The migration created `CREATE TYPE "PhotoEventType" AS ENUM ('impression','tap')` and `photo_events.event_type` IS that enum in the database (information_schema: udt_name = PhotoEventType). schema.prisma declares `eventType String @map("event_type") @db.VarChar(16)`. Reads/writes work (Postgres coerces a text literal into the enum), so this is invisible at runtime — but (a) the TypeScript type is `string`, so the enum's closed domain is unenforced in app code, and (b) `prisma migrate diff` reports `Column event_type would be dropped and recreated (type changed)` — a `prisma migrate dev` would generate a DESTRUCTIVE column drop/recreate on the photo-events ledger. Same class, lower severity: PhotoEvent carries no `photo` relation field at all, so the DB's real `photo_events_photo_id_fkey` (ON DELETE CASCADE, added by 20260710140000_photo_events_fk after deleting orphans) is invisible to Prisma too. | ESCALATED (design proposal — align schema.prisma to the DB, no data migration needed) | information_schema.columns + pg_constraint on replay DB + prisma migrate diff

F303 | api-migrations | 20260802060000_timestamptz_everywhere/migration.sql | **THE HEAVIEST REWRITE IN THE CORPUS LACKS THE SERIAL-PLAN GUARD.** 189 lines converting 162 columns from `timestamp` to `timestamptz` across most tables — every one a full table rewrite plus a rebuild of that table's indexes. `SET max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 0; SET max_parallel_maintenance_workers = 0;` appears 0 times in it. The prod /dev/shm lesson (CLAUDE.md; learned 2026-08-02) is applied in five siblings — 20260801230000_attribute_identity_unique, 20260801240000_edge_hygiene, 20260802010000_unicode_fold, 20260802030000_empty_fold_guard, 20260802050000_identity_key_app_written — and this migration is TIMESTAMPED LATER than all of them, so the omission is an oversight, not a predecessor. It has already applied successfully on the local dev DB (`_prisma_migrations` shows it finished) so it is not a live emergency; the hazard is a future fresh prod-shaped restore or a replay against a large corpus on the small-/dev/shm container. Sibling 20260802170000_in_scoring_territory (a corpus-wide UPDATE over core_restaurant_locations) is also unguarded. NOT EDITABLE — applied history. Recorded as a CRAVE.md gotcha for future migration authors. | OWNER-DECISION (gotcha only; no edit possible) | grep -c max_parallel across all 215 files + local \_prisma_migrations state

F304 | api-migrations | prisma/schema.prisma vs migration-sum | **DRIFT INVENTORY — the migration corpus owns raw-SQL objects Prisma cannot model, and `migrate dev` will try to DROP them every time.** `prisma migrate diff --from-migrations --to-schema-datamodel` exits 2 with a documented delta. Triaged into three classes. (1) PRISMA-UNMODELABLE, deliberate, must never be "fixed" in schema.prisma: the HNSW vector index on core_entities.name_embedding; three partial/expression indexes on core_entities (type+status+enrichment_failure_count, type+identity_key, type+identity_key_sorted); the `places` partial unique on provider_place_id; the `curated_lists` NULLS-NOT-DISTINCT unique; place_geometries.geometry (PostGIS type, plus its GiST index) — Prisma has no geometry type, so it reports the COLUMN as removed; and the `signals` primary key, which must be (signal_id, occurred_at) because Postgres requires the partition key in every unique constraint on a partitioned table. (2) COSMETIC: index-name renames on signal_demand_daily, signals, probed_regions, spend_campaigns, vendor_lookup_misses, poll_creation_attempts (raw-SQL names vs Prisma's derived names). (3) REAL: F301 (orphaned enums) and F302 (photo_events.event_type). This is the SAME root cause already documented in-file at 20260705003434_recreate_entity_name_embedding_hnsw, where a `migrate dev` drift-diff silently dropped the HNSW index inside an UNRELATED poll migration (20260618201804) and it had to be recreated. That in-file warning is correct and generalizes to every item in class (1). | OWNER-DECISION (gotcha; classes 1+2 are correct-as-is) | prisma migrate diff --from-migrations --to-schema-datamodel --shadow-database-url (exit 2), full output triaged

F305 | api-migrations | 20260720110000_signals_monthly_partitions + apps/api/src/modules/signals/signal-partition-maintenance.service.ts | **THE SIGNALS LEDGER'S WRITE AVAILABILITY DEPENDS ON A CRON THAT A GLOBAL FLAG CAN KILL.** The migration creates partitions only through `signals_p2026_10` (verified on the replay DB: bounds stop at 2026-11-01), plus `signals_p_pre` for MINVALUE→2026-06-01. Beyond that, partitions are minted by SignalPartitionMaintenanceService's `@Cron('10 3 * * *')` keeping [current .. current+2] present. The service's own header states the stakes correctly: an insert into a month with no partition FAILS, and the §3 law makes the writer swallow write failures — so the failure mode is SILENTLY DROPPED SIGNALS, not an error. Two couplings the migration cannot express: (a) `CRONS_ENABLED=false` (src/shared/utils/process-role.ts:46) is a GLOBAL cron kill-switch that disables ScheduleModule entirely — it takes partition maintenance with it, so a flag set for cost/lane reasons silently disarms ledger write availability; (b) a database created FRESH after 2026-11-01 (new staging, a restore, a replay) has no partition for "now" until the first 03:10 pass fires. Note this compounds F205 (partition-maintenance cron fails log-only, no ops_alert) already recorded by another territory. | OWNER-DECISION (gotcha; overlaps F205/D21) | pg_class relpartbound on replay DB + read of signal-partition-maintenance.service.ts + grep CRONS_ENABLED

F306 | api-migrations | migration directory names (8 same-timestamp groups) | **SAME-TIMESTAMP PREFIXES ARE RESOLVED BY DIRECTORY-NAME ALPHABETICAL ORDER, AND THAT TIEBREAK IS LOAD-BEARING.** Eight groups share a timestamp prefix: 20260708030000 (access_grants | collection_schedules), 20260726120000 (curated_lists | lists_rename_and_favorites_kind), 20260801120000 (drop_fallback_identity_index | user_layer_restrict_fks), 20260801130000 (llm_prompts | place_geometries_fetched_at_index), 20260801140000 (coverage_claims | probed_regions_view_index | runs_prompt_dedup — a THREE-way tie), 20260802020000 (identity_key_sorted | vendor_lookup_memory), 20260802030000 (empty_fold_guard | poll_creation_attempts), 20260802040000 (enrichment_failure_count | polls_hot_path_indexes). Prisma sorts by full directory name, so ties break on the suffix — deterministic and stable across machines, and the F300 replay proves the resulting order is dependency-correct today (notably 20260801130000_llm_prompts sorts BEFORE 20260801140000_runs_prompt_dedup, which INSERTs into llm_prompts — that one is protected by the differing timestamp, not the tiebreak). The hazard is prospective: a future migration authored with a colliding timestamp whose suffix sorts BEFORE a sibling it depends on would replay-fail on a fresh DB while succeeding on every already-migrated one. | IDEAL-VERIFIED as history (replay-proven); gotcha for future authors | ls + name-sort analysis, confirmed by the F300 end-to-end replay

F307 | api-migrations | migration-sum vs schema.prisma (table census) | **NO DEAD TABLES — a clean result.** Every one of the 97 base tables the corpus builds on an empty DB is accounted for in schema.prisma, except exactly four expected non-app classes: `_prisma_migrations` (Prisma's own), `spatial_ref_sys` (PostGIS), and the six `signals_p*` RANGE partitions (Prisma models the partitioned PARENT only — correct). The corpus is unusually disciplined about this: table deaths are explicit and reasoned (20260514215500 entity_priority_metrics, 20260517165000 display_rank_scores, 20260709040000 billing_entitlements/checkout_sessions, 20260719230000 collection_schedules, 20260720030000 the seven-table Phase C purge, 20260722120000 the four market tables, 20260723130000 user_favorite_events/user_events, 20260725190000 user_favorites, 20260628000000 core_public_entity_score_history). The dead-schema residue in this corpus is TYPE-level (F301), not table-level. | IDEAL-VERIFIED | information_schema.tables on replay DB cross-checked against schema.prisma
